英業達(2356 TT) Trading Buy

產能及訂單展望正向，投資建議 Trading Buy 
投資建議：我們預估隨零組件的產能逐漸提升，以及商用筆電
換機潮和 AI PC 的需求帶動下，2027 年營收將可年增 47.9%，
然而近期市場對 AI 基礎建設投資的疑慮升溫，可能限制英業達
未來營收上行。綜上所述，我們給予英業達 Trading Buy 的投資
建議，目標價 59 元。 
10/2026 營收 799.66 億元，YoY+46.02%：英業達 10/2026 營收
799.66 億元，MoM-18.93%，YoY+46.02%，較我們預期的 849.20
億元差異約 5.8%，大致符合預期。 
3Q26 營收 2,720.94 億元，QoQ+0.8%： 3Q26 營收 2,720.94 億
元，QoQ+0.8%，YoY+54.3%；營益率 1.61%；業外淨支出 13.25
億元；歸屬母公司淨利 25.38 億元，稅後 EPS 0.71。EPS 季減主
因為業外淨支出。 
PC 市場需求疲弱：管理層認為 2026 年為此波循環低點，預估
2027 年全球 PC 市場將呈年增持平至微幅增長，因此可期待 2027
年的 Windows 換機潮以及 AI PC 的需求增長帶動 PC 營收增長。 
預估 4Q26 營收 2,840.78 億元，稅後 EPS 0.80 元：我們認為
4Q26 供應端長短料問題尚未顯著改善，且 PC 市場維持疲弱，
因此預估 4Q26 營收 2,840.78 億元，QoQ+4.4%；毛利率 4.18%；
歸屬母公司淨利 28.75 億元，QoQ+13.3%，稅後 EPS 0.80 元。 
預估 2027 年營收 15,179.62 億元，稅後 EPS 4.90 元：我們預期
2027 年零組件供需失衡狀況將好轉，在既有專案不變下，伺服
器出貨量將可望年增高個位數，且隨伺服器出貨模式逐漸轉為
L11 乃至 L12，將帶動營收顯著成長，預估 2027 年營收 15,179.62
億元，YoY+47.9%；毛利率 4.09%，YoY-0.3ppts；營益率 1.56%，
YoY-0.1ppts，毛利率及營益率衰減主要受產品組合改變所致；
歸屬母公司淨利 175.61 億元，稅後 EPS 4.90 元，YoY+49.6%。

2 群益投顧 
  ESG 
環境：公司於 2026 年通過科學基礎減碳目標倡議（SBTi）淨零目標審核，
積極推動節能減碳、再生能源使用及資源循環。依公司揭露，2025 年共執
行 29 項節能方案，節省約 792 萬度電，廢棄物回收再利用率達 80.3%。此
外，公司持續推動綠色產品設計，2025 年取得 861 件產品環保及能效認證，
並透過導入回收材料、降低包裝塑膠使用量及研發伺服器液冷技術，降低產
品生命週期之環境衝擊。 
社會：公司重視員工權益、人才培育及職業安全衛生，持續推動多元共融與
友善職場。2025 年員工平均教育訓練時數達 97.28 小時，員工參與度調查
回收率為 80.9%，並投入約 1,186 萬元於社會參與活動，透過生態保育、志
工服務及公益計畫履行企業社會責任。 
公司治理：英業達透過董事會及永續發展相關組織推動 ESG 管理，強化誠
信經營、資訊透明及供應鏈風險控管。2025 年女性主管占比達 31.6%，供應
商稽核結案率達 100%，違反誠信經營受裁罰案件為零件。此外，公司於 2025
年完成 6.25 億美元 ESG 永續連結聯合授信案，將永續績效指標與融資條件
連結，資金主要用於擴充 AI 伺服器產能及海外製造布局，兼顧營運成長與
永續發展。 
公司簡介 
英業達（2356 TT）為全球主要電子產品設計製造服務（ODM）廠商之一，
核心業務涵蓋個人電腦及企業級運算設備。 公司成立於 1975 年，並 於 1997
年在台灣證券交易所上市，早期以電子產品代工為主要業務，1988 年進軍
筆記型電腦市場，1998 年進一步拓展伺服器領域，逐步建立個人電腦與企
業電腦雙事業布局。 
個人產品事業主要提供筆記型電腦及桌上型電腦之研發設計與製造服務；
企業電腦事業則涵蓋一般伺服器、AI 伺服器、儲存設備、網路交換器及機
櫃級解決方案，應用於企業資料中心、雲端資料中心及主機代管中心。公司
具備從伺服器主機板至整機櫃之設計與製造能力，並提供 ODM、JDM 及 CM
等多元合作模式，滿足不同客戶的產品開發與生產需求。 
在全球製造布局方面，英業達於台灣、中國、墨西哥及捷克等地設有企業電
腦製造營運據點，具備接單生產（BTO）及接單客製化（CTO）能力。隨著
雲端運算、AI 及資料中心基礎建設需求持續發展，公司積極布局 AI 伺服
器、機櫃整合與先進散熱技術，並透過全球產能配置及研發設計能力，強化
產品競爭力與供應鏈韌性。 
10/2026 營收 799.66 億元，YoY+46.02%：英業達 10/2026 營收 799.66 億元，
MoM-18.93%， YoY+46.02%，較我們預期的 849.20 億元差異約 5.8%，大致
符合預期。營收月減主因為 PC 市場需求仍未見好轉，且伺服器端受供應鏈
長短料問題影響，導致出貨狀況不如預期。 
3Q26 營收 2,720.94 億元，QoQ+0.8%：3Q26 營收 2,720.94 億元，QoQ+0.8%，
YoY+54.3%；毛利率 4.17%， QoQ-0.07ppts，營收季增部分原因為零組件漲
價帶動 ASP 提升，然而受此影響毛利率因此遭稀釋。此外，產品組合的改
變亦使毛利率承壓；營業利益 43.73 億元，QoQ-7.8%，YoY+42.6%，營益率

### Page 3

個股報告 
                                        2026 年 11 月 11 日

3 群益投顧 
1.61%；業外淨支出 13.25 億元；歸屬母公司淨利 25.38 億元，稅後 EPS 0.71，
QoQ-35.1%。 EPS 季減主要受業外淨支出所累。 
營運規模擴大及長短料問題，營運成本上升：管理層表示 3Q26 利息費用
22.15 億元，較 3Q25 的 14.76 億元大幅增加 7.39 億元。此外，受供應鏈端
長短料問題影響導致出貨遞延，以及零組件漲價造成營運壓力增加，3Q26
存貨年增 112.3%至 1,624.59 億元，帶動存貨週轉天數年增 7 天至 46.2 天。
目前大部分存貨為原料及零組件，成品庫存占比極低，長料主要為短期積
壓，目前沒有存貨跌價損失的風險，4Q26 營運上仍需視上游供給狀況是否
好轉。管理層另表示，目前的資金需求主要透過短期及中長期的銀行額度融
通，儘管 2027 年營運規模提升帶動資金需求顯著增加，但仍有信心以非股
權稀釋的管道進行籌資。 
PC 市場需求疲弱：記憶體漲價及 CPU 缺貨等問題導致 2026 年 PC 市場疲
弱。受 1H26 提前拉貨效應及零組件漲價的影響，縱然 3Q26 為傳統旺季，
英業達 PC 出貨量仍季減高個位數。然而據我們了解，英業達的 PC 出貨以
需求彈性較高的商用為主，約佔 PC 總出貨量的 60-70%，因此在 ASP 提高
及出貨量衰退的影響下營收仍可實現增長。管理層認為 2026 年為此波循環
低點，預估 2027 年全球 PC 市場將呈年增持平至微幅增長，因此可期待 2027
年的 Windows 換機潮以及 AI PC 的需求增長帶動 PC 營收增長。 
2027 年智慧裝置展望樂觀：目前在智慧裝置的營運重心為無線通訊模組、
AIoT 以及 Edge AI，其中無線通訊模組部分布局 4G、5G 通訊，並規劃未來
推進高速有線通訊模組領域。2026 年智慧裝置原物料漲幅極高，然而目前
供應鏈端狀況已初見好轉，預估 2027 年漲幅趨緩，出貨量將呈個位數增長。
AI 資料中心全年展望不變：10/2026 美國期中選舉結果引發市場對資料中心
建置的疑慮，然而英業達表示已與客戶確認訂單並未出現調整，CSP 需求仍
強勁，惟 Neocloud 的通用型伺服器需求受其資金排擠效果影響較大，短期
的出貨量可能會有波動。英業達的德州廠主要是供應全美市場，而非僅針對
德州，因此受資料中心相關法案影響較小，並將會持續發展全球布局。 
伺服器營收將顯著成長：英業達目前伺服器營收仍以 GPU 及 ASIC 系列為
主。目前 Nvidia 的 Vera Rubin 將以 L11 及 L12 為主，HGX 系列則主要出
L10 和 L11；AMD 系列則以 L6 的形式出貨；ASIC 部分則是個別專案而定，，
整體以 L6 或 L10 出貨。管理層預期 4Q26 伺服器出貨量將持平 3Q26，成長
性主要還是受長短料問題壓抑，客戶端需求及產能方面都維持樂觀。我們認
為 2027 年英業達 GPU/ASIC 伺服器出貨量將呈高雙位數增長，且將重心由
過往的 L6 逐漸轉往 L10 至 L12 的組裝，成為營收的主要成長動能，但也因
此預期毛利率/營益率將難以維持 5%/2%的表現。 
預估 4Q26 營收 2,840.78 億元，稅後 EPS 0.80 元：我們認為 4Q26 供應端長
短料問題尚未顯著改善，且 PC 市場將維持疲弱，因此預估 4Q26 營收
2,840.78 億元，QoQ+4.4%；毛利率 4.18%；營益率 1.67%；歸屬母公司淨利
28.75 億元，QoQ+13.3%，稅後 EPS 0.80 元。 
預估 2027 年營收 15,179.62 億元，稅後 EPS 4.90 元：我們預期 2027 年記憶
體、CPU、PCB 及被動元件等零組件供需失衡狀況將好轉，在既有專案不變
下，伺服器出貨量將可望年增高個位數，且隨伺服器出貨模式逐漸轉為 L11
乃至 L12，將帶動營收顯著成長，預估 2027 年營收 15,179.62 億元，

4 群益投顧 
YoY+47.9%；毛利率 4.09%， YoY-0.3ppts；營業利益 237.32 億元，營益率
1.56%，YoY-0.1ppts，毛利率及營益率衰減主要受產品組合改變所致，然而
毛利淨額/營業利益仍可年增 38.6%/35.8%；歸屬母公司淨利 175.61 億元，
稅後 EPS 4.90 元，YoY+49.6%。  
投資建議： 2H26 英業達受限於 PC 市場的疲弱及供應鏈端的長短料問題，
營收成長速度放緩。我們預估隨著記憶體、CPU、PCB 及被動元件等零組件
的產能逐漸提升，以及在商用筆電換機潮和 AI PC 的需求帶動下，此一情景
可望在 2027 年逐漸緩解。此外，英業達已向客戶確認伺服器展望維持正向，
既有訂單將不會有變動，CSP 的需求仍舊強勁。惟近期市場對 CSP 巨頭的
現金流擔憂上升，未來 Neocloud 及 Enterprise 的需求不確定性較高，且 AI
資料中心的建置亦可能受未來相關法案所限而推遲，可能限制英業達營收
上行。綜上所述，我們給予英業達 Trading Buy 的投資建議，目標價 59 元。

8 群益投顧 
  【投資評等說明】 
  評等 定義 
  強力買進(Strong Buy)  首次評等潛在上漲空間≥ 35%  
  買進(Buy)  15% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間<35%  
  區間操作(Trading Buy) 5%≤首次評等潛在上漲空間<15%

中立(Neutral)  
無法由基本面給予投資評等 
預期近期股價將處於盤整 
建議降低持股

【投資評等調整機制】 
  投資評等係「首次」給予特定個股投資評等時，其前一日收盤價相較 3 個月
目標價之潛在上漲空間計算而得。個股投資評等分為四個等級，定義如上。
爾後的投資評等係依循「首次評等」，直到停止推薦。  
「強力買進」、「買進」及「區間操作」均有 upside 目標價。差別僅在於，
首次評等時潛在報酬率不同。「中立」則無目標價。 
一旦我們給予特定個股「強力買進」、「買進」或「區間操作」之投資評等，
就是責任的開始，爾後將透過各式研究報告作定期性、持續性追蹤基本面及
股價變化，直到停止推薦。 
停止推薦情境： 
1. 達目標價。 
2. 雖未達原訂目標價，但檢視基本面、訊息面、籌碼面等多方訊息，研
判股價上漲空間已然有限，將適時出具降評報告。 
3. 推薦後股價不漲反跌。

【免責聲明】 
  本研究報告僅提供予特定人之客戶作為參考資料「非經同意不得轉載」。我
們並不確保此資訊的完整性與正確性，投資人應了解，報告中有關未來預測
之陳述可能不會實現，因而不應被依賴。而且此報告並非根據特定投資目的
或依預定對象之財務狀況所撰寫出來的，因此，此研究報告的目的，既非對
投資人於買賣證券、選擇權、期貨或其他證券相關之衍生性商品提供詢價服
務，亦非作為進行交易的要約。投資人應注意到相關證券之價值及收益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

More details on SBC — SBC's 9M26 sales declined by a single digit y/y with stable 
GPM and improving opex. Pre-tax profits rose 40% y/y in 9M26. Yet the higher 
effective tax rate dragged profitability, leading to a  net loss of US$25mn in 9M26. 
Mgmt expects SBC to launch more new products in 2027E which should further 
drive the top-line and earnings recovery.  
Implications — We expect a negative share price reaction to the results print given 
the weaker-than-expected core biz and the SBC loss. Mgmt expects FY26 sales to 
fall 14-15% y/y with 4Q OEM sales seasonally softer than 3Q. New models are due 
from Jan-27 and mgmt expects low-teens growth y/y for FY27E. We will look for 
more detail on the drivers of the expected sales recovery at its 3Q26 results briefing 
next Tuesday, Nov 17.

3
Merida
Valuation
Our target price for Merida shares of NT$93 is based on a 2H26-1H27E P/E of 15.5x, a 30% discount to its historical average of
22x during pre-Covid years (2014-2019), in view of soft order momentum as a result of slow recovery in bike demand, a volatile
margin trend, and low visibility of SBC.
Risks
Downside risks that could mean the Merida shares trade below our target price include: 1) e-bike adoption slower than

expected; 2) weaker restocking demand; 3) lukewarm sell-through at SBC; and 4) a weaker macro.
Upside risks that could mean the Merida shares trade above our target price include: 1) e-bike adoption faster than expected;
2) stronger restocking demand; 3) stronger sell-through at SBC; and 4) a strong macro.

Guide to Citi Research Fundamental Research Investment Ratings:
Citi Research stock recommendations include an investment rating and an optional risk rating to highlight high risk stocks.

Risk rating takes into account both price volatility and fundamental criteria. Stocks will either have no risk rating or a High risk
rating assigned.
Investment Ratings: Citi Research investment ratings are Buy, Neutral and Sell. Our ratings are a function of analyst
expectations of expected total return ("ETR") and risk. ETR is the sum of the forecast price appreciation (or depreciation) plus
the dividend yield for a stock within the next 12 months.  The target price is based on a 12 month time horizon. The Investment

rating definitions are: Buy (1) ETR of 15% or more or 25% or more for High risk stocks; and Sell (3) for negative ETR. Any covered
stock not assigned a Buy or a Sell is a Neutral (2). For stocks rated Neutral (2), if an analyst believes that there are insufficient
valuation drivers and/or investment catalysts to derive a positive or negative investment view, they may elect with the approval
of Citi Research management not to assign a target price and, thus, not derive an ETR. Citi Research may suspend its rating

and target price and assign “Rating Suspended” status for regulatory and/or internal policy reasons. Citi Research may also
suspend its rating and target price and assign "Under Review" status for other exceptional circumstances (e.g. lack of
information critical to the analyst's thesis, trading suspension) affecting the company and/or trading in the company's
securities. In both such situations, the rating and target price will show as “—“ and “-“ respectively in the rating history price

chart. Prior to 11 April 2022 Citi Research assigned “Under Review” status to both situations and prior to 11 Nov 2020 only in
exceptional circumstances. As soon as practically possible, the analyst will publish a note re-establishing a rating and
investment thesis. Investment ratings are determined by the ranges described above at the time of initiation of coverage, a
change in investment and/or risk rating, or a change in target price (subject to limited management discretion). At times, the

expected total returns may fall outside of these ranges because of market price movements and/or other short-term volatility
or trading patterns. Such interim deviations will be permitted but will become subject to review by Research Management.
Your decision to buy or sell a security should be based upon your personal investment objectives and should be made only
after evaluating the stock's expected performance and risk.
Catalyst Watch/Short Term Views (“STV”) Ratings Disclosure:

Catalyst Watch and STV Upside/Downside calls: Citi Research may also include a Catalyst Watch or STV Upside or Downside
call to indicate the analyst expects the share price to rise (fall) in absolute terms over a specified period of 30 or 90 days in
reaction to one or more specific near-term catalysts or events impacting the company or the market. A Catalyst Watch will be
published when Analyst confidence is high that an impact to share price will occur; it will be a STV when confidence level is

moderate. A Catalyst Watch or STV Upside/Downside call will automatically expire at the end of the specified 30/90 day
period. The Catalyst Watch will also be automatically removed if share price performance (calculated at market close) exceeds
15% against the direction of the call (unless over-ridden by the analyst). The analyst may also remove a Catalyst Watch or STV
call prior to the end of the specified period in a published research note. A Catalyst Watch/STV Upside or Downside call may be

buy recommendation and a Catalyst Watch/STV Downside call corresponds to a sell recommendation. Any stock not assigned

## Document 261: [329180] HD현대중공업(329180) 실적&임단협 우려 해소, 발전엔진 수요 폭발적HD현대중공업(329180) 실적&임단협 우려 해소, 발전엔진 수요 폭발_5484f7f5

Source PDF: all report pdf/[329180] HD현대중공업(329180) 실적&임단협 우려 해소, 발전엔진 수요 폭발적HD현대중공업(329180) 실적&임단협 우려 해소, 발전엔진 수요 폭발_5484f7f5.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-10-29
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

HD 현대중공업 (329180/KS) 
실적&임단협 우려 해소, 발전엔진 수요 폭발적 
2026-10-29

조선/방산. 한승한, R.A 고서영 / shane.han@sks.co.kr / 3773-9992

매수(유지) 
목표주가: 640,000 원(하향) 
현재주가: 285,500 원 
상승여력: 124.2%

Signal: 3Q26, 조업일수 감소 및 환율 하락으로 시장 예상치 하회 
Key: 4Q26~1Q27 부터 본격적인 발전엔진 및 FDC 수주 모멘텀 전망 
Step: 상선 우려 감안해도 엔진&FDC 으로 밸류 매력도 충분. 매수 기회

3Q26 Review: 시장예상치 하회하는 실적 시현  
3Q26 연결 매출액 5조 7,536억원(+30.2% YoY, -9.1% QoQ), 영업이익 8,992억원
(+61.3% YoY, -13.5% QoQ)으로, 시장 예상치(9,792억원)를 하회했다. 조업일수 감소
(휴가 10 일+중대재해 4일) 및 환율 하락 영향(450 억원)과 더불어, 임단협 관련 예상 비
용 중 75%를 이번 분기 선반영 했기 때문이다. 상선 수주 빈티지는 중형 선박 기준 ‘23 년 
9.6%, ‘24년 65%, ‘25년 25%, 대형 선박 기준 ‘22년 4%, ‘23년 45%, ‘24년 31%, 
‘25년 20%로, 선가 상승효과 지속되는 중이다. 특히 해양 부문에서 루야 프로젝트의 공정
률 확대 효과 및 C/O로 180억원의 환입 발생으로 전 분기 대비 큰 폭의 이익 성장 시현했
다. 최근 키윗과 미국 내 합작 조선소 설립을 추진하며, 미 군수지원함 초도 2척 수주 및 향
후 물량 건조, 그리고 HII와의 함정 선체블록 하도급 사업 수주까지 기대할 수 있다.

투자의견 ‘매수’ 유지, 목표주가 64 만원으로 하향 
HD 현대중공업에 대한 투자의견 ‘매수’를 유지하고, 목표주가는 기존 82 만원에서 64 만원
으로 하향한다. 최근 상선 실적 피크아웃 우려에 따른 조선주 주가 급락으로 멀티플 하향 조
정했다. 하지만 상선 부문의 우려를 감안하더라도 상선 사이클과 무관한 육상 발전 엔진 및 
FDC 에 대한 글로벌 하이퍼스케일러들의 수요 확대는 지속되고 있는 중이다. 올해 공시 기
준으로 육상발전 엔진 약 1.6GW 수주한 것으로 알려졌으나, 미공시된 약 1.4GW 까지 포
함 시 총 3.0GW 이상을 수주한 상황이다. 캐파 증설 기준으로 ‘28 년 1.5GW, ‘29 년 
3GW 캐파에 해당하는 물량을 이미 대부분 채웠으며, 기존 계획 대비 빠른 준공 혹은 공정
률 확대, 더 나아가 추가적인 캐파 증설까지 고려할 수준으로 파악된다. 빠르면 올해 연말 
혹은 내년 초부터 4 행정 중속엔진&FDC 본격 수주를 통해 중장기적 신성장 동력 확보할 
전망이다. 현 주가 레벨에서 상승여력 충분히 남아있다는 판단으로, 3Q 실적 및 임단협 불
확성 해소된 현 시점에서 적극적 매수 의견 및 조선주 최선호주(Top-Pick)로 제시한다.

## Document 266: [373220] LG에너지솔루션(373220) ESS 수주 모멘텀 이어진다LG에너지솔루션(373220) ESS 수주 모멘텀 이어진다ESS 수주 모멘텀 이어진다 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[373220] LG에너지솔루션(373220) ESS 수주 모멘텀 이어진다LG에너지솔루션(373220) ESS 수주 모멘텀 이어진다ESS 수주 모멘텀 이어진다 - 기업.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-11-04
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

2026.11.04   
LG에너지솔루션 
(373220)

ESS 수주 모멘텀 이어진다 Company Brief

3Q26 실적, 원-달러 환율 하락에도 시장 기대치 대폭 상회 
동사의 3Q26 실적은 매출 9.6조원(+59% YoY, +28% QoQ), 영업이익 7,560억원 
(+26% YoY, +568% QoQ)으로 원/달러 환율 하락에도 불구하고 시장기대치(매출 
8.8조원, 영업이익 3,217억원)를 크게 상회했다. 당사는 Stellantis와의 캐나다 
합작관계 종료에 따른 보상금 등을 포함해 약 4,250억원의 일회성 이익이 반영된 
것으로 추정한다. 이를 제외한 영업이익은 약 3,310억원으로, 시장기대치에 대체로 
부합한 것으로 판단된다. AMPC는 미국 ESS 생산량 증가와 GM 얼티엄셀즈 재가동에 
힘입어 전분기 대비 약 73% 증가한 4,169억원을 기록했다. 사업부문별로는 ESS의 
실적 개선이 두드러졌다. 상반기 배터리 패키징 병목으로 이연됐던 물량 출하가 
집중되면서 ESS 출하량은 전분기 대비 60% 가량 증가한 것으로 추정된다. 자동차 
전지도 유럽향 물량 회복과 GM 얼티엄셀즈 재가동으로 매출이 전분기 대비 약 
33%로 증가했으며, 고정비 부담 완화에 따라 적자폭도 뚜렷하게 축소된 것으로 
판단된다. 소형전지는 주요 고객사인 Tesla의 판매 호조에 따른 출하 증가로 매출 
회복과 수익성을 뒷받침한 것으로 보인다. 
4Q26 ESS 중심의 수익성 개선과 수주 모멘텀 지속될 전망 
미국 ESS 시장은 신재생 에너지 확대와 AI 데이터센터의 전력 수요 증가에 힘입어 
빠르게 성장하고 있다. 특히 정책 환경도 비PFE 공급망을 확보한 업체에 유리하게 
작용하고 있다. 동사는 3Q26 실적 컨퍼런스 콜에서 ESS 수주잔고가 약 
160GWh이며, 2028년까지 캐파의 대부분에 대응하는 물량을 확보했다고 밝혔다. 
또한 현재 다수의 고객사들과 2028년 이후 공급 물량을 협의하고 있어 연말까지 
추가적인 수주 모멘텀이 이어질 것으로 예상된다. 이를 바탕으로 4Q26에도 동사의 
본업 실적은 ESS 중심의 개선세를 이어갈 전망이다. 전분기에 반영된 대규모 일회성 
이익의 기저효과로 영업이익은 감소하겠지만, 이를 제외하면 출하량 증가와 가동률 
상승에 따른 점진적인 수익성 개선이 예상된다. 수익성 중심의 사업 효율화를 위해 
소형 IT용 파우치 사업을 축소하고 있다는 점도 중장기 손익 개선에 긍정적이다. 이를 
반영한 4Q26 매출과 영업이익은 각각 약 10.5조원(+63% YoY, +9% QoQ), 
4,150억원(흑자전환 YoY, -45% QoQ)으로 추정된다. 
매수 투자의견과 목표주가 유지 
동사에 대한 매수 투자의견과 목표주가를 유지한다. 북미 ESS 수요 증가와 공급망 
재편에 따른 신규 수주 확대가 중장기 실적 성장을 뒷받침할 전망이다. 특히 ESS용 
생산라인 전환과 가동률 상승이 본격화되면 매출 증가에 더해 고정비 부담 완화와 
AMPC 수혜 확대가 맞물리면서 수익성도 개선될 것으로 예상된다. ESS의 역할이 
신재생 에너지 연계용 저장장치를 넘어 AI 데이터센터의 안정적인 전력 공급을 
지원하는 핵심 인프라로 확대되고 있다는 점에도 주목할 필요가 있다. 이에 따라 
동사의 밸류에이션 역시 기존 EV 중심의 평가에서 벗어나 전력 인프라 산업의 
성장성을 반영하는 방향으로 재평가되어야 한다. 업종 내 실적 개선 가시성과 정책 
수혜가 뚜렷하고, 4Q26 신규 수주에 따른 주가 상승 가능성도 높다는 점에서 동사에 
대한 비중 확대 전략이 유효하다는 판단이다.

당사의 허가 없이 전재, 복사 또는 대여될 수 없습니다. 무단전재 등으로 인한 분쟁발생시 법적 책임이 있음을 주지하시기 바랍니다.

Units lift the headline
2 November 2026
Key points
• Total dwelling approvals rose 5.1%mth
in September, although the annual pace
moved into contraction at –0.8%yr with
the detail suggesting the underlying
cycle is starting to turn down.

• The headline increase was driven by
another volatile rise in private unit
approvals (+16.6%) which more than
offset a significant decline in private
detached house approvals (–5.3%).
• Units drove gains in NSW and
Queensland. WA, SA and Victoria all
recorded declines. Detached dwelling
approvals fell across all states.
• The total value of residential approvals
fell 2.8%mth but a sharp rise in non-
residential approvals – led by the
data centres – saw the total value of

building approvals rise 13%mth.
• Interest rate increases, weakness in
established housing markets, rising costs
and pessimistic consumer sentiment
are likely to remain headwinds.

### Page 2

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
Total dwelling approvals rose 5.1%mth in September, while
the annual pace moved into contraction at –0.8%yr. The
result was well above both Westpac’s expectation for a
7.0%mth decline and the market expectation for a 2.0%mth
rise. Unit approvals continued to drive headline volatility,

surging in September, while detached dwellings, which
provide a better read on the underlying trend, recorded a
sizeable decline.
Note that most figures in the charts and table are presented on
a rolling-three month basis to smooth out monthly volatility.
Private detached dwelling approvals fell 5.3%mth in
September, unwinding August’s upwardly-revised 5.4%mth
rise (+1.7ppts). Recent resilience has kept the segment
elevated, with approvals remaining above 10,000 and the

annual pace at 9.6%yr. However, the quarterly profile provides
a clearer read on the underlying trend and suggests detached
dwelling approvals are beginning to respond to recent interest
rate increases (see here). Approvals rose just 0.9%qtr in
September vs 3.1%qtr in June and 5.9%qtr in March.
Private unit approvals remained volatile, rising 16.6%mth in
September following 21.7%mth decline in August. Strength
was led by high-rise approvals, while low-mid-rise approvals

recorded a more modest increase. Despite the monthly rise,
the annual pace fell further into contraction at –20.5%yr.
Among the states, NSW recorded the strongest result,
with private approvals rising 35.3%mth led by a surge in
private unit approvals. Queensland also recorded an increase
(+9.3%mth), while WA (–2.9%mth), SA (–3.5%mth) and
Victoria (–29.0%mth) all declined.
In value terms, residential building approvals fell 2.8%mth,
driven by a 3.3%mth decline in new residential approvals,

partially offset by a 0.8%mth rise in renovation activity.
Non-residential approvals once again reversed sharply, rising
45.9%mth, as the data centre-dominated ‘other commercial’
segment recorded an 8-fold increase on a non-seasonally
adjusted basis. The total value of building approvals rose
13.0%mth in September. On a non-seasonally adjusted basis,
the value of building approvals rose 8.3%mth overall, but
declined 3.0%mth when excluding the ‘other commercial’

segment.
September’s result reinforces the importance of looking
through headline volatility in unit approvals. While total
approvals rose in the month, detached dwelling approvals
slowed on a quarterly basis and fell across all states, pointing
to softer underlying momentum. The annual pace of total
approvals also moved into contraction for the first time
since January. Interest rate increases, the sharp downturn
in established housing markets and rising costs are likely to

weigh on further growth, with pessimistic consumer sentiment
an additional headwind (see here).
Detached approvals point to softer momentum

1 October 2026
Key points
• The Cotality home value index – covering
all of Australia – fell 1.2% in October
following a 1.3% decline in September

running at –2.8%yr with prices down
6.7% from their peak in March.
• Across the major cities, Brisbane and

Australian dwelling prices

### Page 2

forecasts are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially
from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The Cotality home value index, covering all of Australia, fell
1.2% in October with slight revisions to previous months
again shaving a further 0.3% off prices. The correction
has now seen prices decline 6.7% from their March peak

with annual growth firmly in negative at –2.8%yr. Turnover
continues fall as well, down 22.5%yr. Housing-related
tax policy changes, interest rate rises and heightened
uncertainty have triggered a sharp contraction in markets.
The downturn is now comparable to the previous two
housing market corrections. Nationally, the downturn in
2022-23 saw prices decline 7.5% and turnover fall 29% peak
to trough. The 2017-18 correction saw prices fall 8.6% and

turnover by 24%, with larger falls in Sydney and Melbourne.
Note that unless specified, figures in titles, tables and
commentary are in non‑seasonally adjusted terms, consistent
with the ‘headline’ figures reported by Cotality. However,
figures shown in charts are in seasonally adjusted terms.
Preliminary estimates of turnover show rolling 3mth volumes
down 5.6%qtr in October, previous estimates again revised
lower. The monthly pattern suggests declines re‑accelerated

through September‑October after stabilising somewhat in July‑
August. The RBA's interest rate rise in late September and a
further expected tightening likely contributed to the shift.
The detail shows Brisbane and Perth are now leading the
way in terms of monthly price declines although corrections
to date remain significantly larger in Sydney and Melbourne
while smaller capitals and regional areas continue to see much
milder downturns. Across price tiers, all segments are now

showing similar rates of decline after top price tiers had seen
a more pronounced and earlier downturn. 'Top tier' segments
have tended to lead recent price cycles.
Looking across jurisdictions,
• Sydney prices dropped 1.6% in October having averaged
similar‑sized declines over the previous five months. While
all segments and tiers continue to record falls, the last few
months has seen the pace of decline moderate a touch in the
top tier segments that have led cycles in the past, although

they are still a long way from stabilisation.
• Melbourne prices declined 0.8%mth, in line with
September's move and continuing the slower pace of
retreat. As noted, this likely reflects the weaker starting
point coming into the correction, prices already at 5½ year
lows. Interestingly, the detail shows the moderation in price
declines in recent months has been more pronounced for
'top tier' units where prices are back where they were in
2016.
Housing downturn deepens

forecasts are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially

3WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
• Brisbane prices are tracking a very fast rate of decline
that is also seeing large downward revisions. Prices fall
2% in October with September and August now both
showing 2.1% falls, revised down from 1.5% and 1.6%

respectively (August having previously been estimated at
just 1%, July's move having seen a similar series of revisions
–1.2% to –1.8%). The combined effect has taken the rolling
3mth change to –6.1%qtr from –4.7%qtr last months and
–2.7%qtr in August and –0.6%qtr in July. At 0.4%yr, annual
price growth has virtually stalled flat having run at 18%yr as
recently as May. All tiers and segments are recording similar
rates of decline.
• Perth is seeing something very similar, prices dropping a

further 1.7% in October, with September's decline revised
down to 2.1% from –1.2% and August's to 2.0% from –1.7%.
At 3.9%yr, annual growth is still positive but off steeply
from the 24% pace back in May. As with Brisbane, all tiers
and segments are recording similar rates of decline.
• Adelaide dwelling prices posted a somewhat milder 1.2%
decline in October, , September's –1.3% decline marked
down to –1.7%. Annual price growth slowed to 3.7%yr,

similar to Perth's but with less of a whiplash from strong
gains to large declines. Turnover has also been much
steadier, sales holding flat over the last three months and
down just 12.4%yr.
• The smaller capitals were again more mixed, price growth
still positive in Darwin (0.6%mth, 9.7%yr), relatively resilient
in Hobart (+0.5%mth, 5.5%yr) but weaker in Canberra
(–0.7%mth, –3.7%yr).
• Regional markets showed a 0.9% decline in Qld but slightly
milder declines in NSW (–0.7%) and Victoria (0.6%). Annual

prices growth is in the 1.4‑3.5%yr range.
All up, the October updates make for bleak reading. With the
RBA widely expected to raise interest rates again, the near
term outlook remains very challenging. Next week's consumer
sentiment update will show how this is shaping housing‑
related sentiment – so far, consumer price expectations have
weakened but stopped short of swinging to the clear negative
consensus that emerged in the 2022‑23 correction. That now

looks to be at risk. Either way, momentum looks set to remain
weak into year‑end and, barring a significantly improved
inflation and interest rate outlook, well into 2027.

### Page 2

Weekly Economics
U.S. Week Ahead
Existing Home Sales • Thursday
Housing market conditions remain highly unfavorable. Mortgage
rates are the primary headwind, and they have climbed even higher
alongside the rise in longer-term yields. At the same time, low
supply is preventing a broad correction in home prices, leaving
prospective buyers squeezed on two fronts.
We expect that existing home sales slid 3.4% in August to an
annualized rate of 3.92 million. Behind this forecast is ongoing

weakness in mortgage applications and a recent bounce in
mortgage rates, which rose from 6.43% at the beginning of July to
6.66% by month-end.
We do not expect affordability conditions to meaningfully improve
in the near term. Supply is likely to remain constrained while high
mortgage rates discourage mobility. And mortgage rates are apt
to remain elevated amid firm economic growth expectations and
heavy government and corporate debt issuance.
(Return)

### Page 3

Economics Week Ahead Economics
CPI • Friday
Headline inflation looks to have picked up last month. We estimate
the CPI rose 0.40% in August, with gasoline prices increasing a little
over 4% as renewed tensions in the Middle East pushed oil higher.
Grocery prices also likely rebounded after modestly declining in July.
Outside of food and energy, price pressures appear little changed.
We expect the core CPI to rise 0.23%, essentially matching July's

pace. Core goods inflation should remain firm, led by another solid
increase in used vehicle prices as consumer prices continue to
catch up to the trend indicated at wholesale auctions. Meantime,
household furnishings and other tariff-sensitive categories appear
to be stabilizing after declining earlier this year.
Core services inflation should be little changed with another 0.2%
monthly increase. But underneath the surface, we expect a modest
pickup in travel (led by lodging away from home) to offset slightly

cooler medical care and housing cost growth.
All told, we expect the report to show that while headline inflation
remains influenced by the ebbs and flows of the conflict in the
Middle East, core inflation remains contained. Should our forecast
prove correct, core CPI will ease to 2.4% year-over-year, in line with
its 6-month annualized rate but above the more recent 3-month
annualized pace of 1.7%.

### Page 4

Weekly Economics
G10 Week Ahead
European Central Bank Monetary Policy Meeting •
Thursday
We expect the European Central Bank (ECB) to raise the Deposit
Rate by 25 bps to 2.50% next week. August headline inflation
remained elevated at 3.3% year-over-year, though softer core
and services inflation suggest underlying pressures continue to
moderate. Still, the more hawkish tone of the July minutes and
renewed rise in energy prices should keep policymakers cautious.

The recent escalation in the Middle East has pushed fuel prices
at the pump close to their 2022 peaks, while natural gas storage
remains below historical levels, leaving the Eurozone vulnerable
to further energy price increases. The ECB is unlikely to respond
aggressively if the shock remains confined to energy given the
downside risks to growth. However, a broader rise in food inflation
linked to El Niño would be more concerning, as highly visible prices

could lift inflation expectations and generate second-round effects.
For now, resilient Q3 activity, elevated headline inflation and
contained wage growth support one final hike. The ECB will likely
retain and emphasize its scenario-based outlook in the updated
staff projections given continued uncertainty around the conflict
and energy prices. The policy path beyond September will depend
on whether energy and food pressures persist and begin to spread,

which could leave room for further tightening.

U.K. Monthly GDP • Friday
We expect UK GDP to rise 0.2% month-over-month in July, with
growth of 0.3% on a three-month-over-three-month basis.

Activity likely moderated after June’s strong rebound, though
services remained supportive as the July services PMI rose to 52.1.
Retail sales volumes fell 0.5% month-over-month, while softer
manufacturing activity likely reflected some reversal of earlier
precautionary stockbuilding.
The moderation appears more consistent with payback from
June than a broader deterioration in momentum. August PMIs
remain consistent with Q3 GDP growth of around 0.3% quarter-

over-quarter. Still, the renewed escalation in the Middle East and
associated rise in gas and energy prices adds upside risk to inflation
and could weigh on household demand. Against this backdrop and
with Bank of England hawks continuing to emphasize the need for
tighter policy, we expect the BoE to remain cautious and continue
to look for a rate hike in Q4. -1.0%

Economics Week Ahead Economics
EM Week Ahead
Mexico CPI • Wednesday
We expect Mexico’s August inflation rate to edge higher, with
headline CPI rising to 3.34% year-over-year (0.27% month-over-
month) from 3.26% year-over-year in early August. Part of the
pickup likely reflects less favorable base effects and a smaller
disinflationary contribution from food prices. While agricultural
prices continued to help restrain inflation in mid-August, the drag

was notably less pronounced than in July. Core inflation should
remain elevated at 3.98% year-over-year (0.25% month-over-
month).
While inflation remains within Banxico’s midpoint 3% target,
persistent core and services inflation suggest convergence toward
the midpoint of the target could prove gradual. For now, we
continue to expect Banxico to keep rates on hold, with the policy
rate remaining at 6.50% through the rest of 2026 and into early
2027 as policymakers await clearer evidence that underlying

inflation pressures are easing sustainably. While rate cuts remain
a possibility in 2027, the current inflation backdrop argues for
patience in the near term.

Brazil CPI • Friday
We expect Brazil’s August inflation rate to slow to 4.20% year-over-
year, helped by lower electricity, food and fuel prices. The main
driver should be a temporary reduction in residential electricity bills
stemming from a one-off credit, while recent data already point to
a notable easing in price pressures. Mid-August CPI came in weaker
than expected, with inflation falling to 4.24% year-over-year amid
declines in electricity, food, fuel and airfare prices. Beyond these

temporary factors, restrictive monetary policy continues to weigh
on economic activity, as evidenced by the recent slowdown in Q2
GDP growth. However, underlying inflation risks remain. A still-
tight labor market and government measures aimed at supporting
consumption could keep services inflation sticky, making progress
toward the 3.0% inflation target more gradual.
Overall, the inflation outlook remains consistent with additional
policy easing. We expect COPOM to deliver a 25 bps rate cut in

September, bringing the Selic Rate to 13.75%. Beyond September,
policymakers are likely to proceed cautiously as they assess
incoming inflation data and the impact of the election cycle on
inflation expectations. Our base case remains for a pause after
September, with additional easing more likely in 2027.

## Document 344: Fed Likely To Hike By 50bps In December

Source PDF: all report pdf/Fed Likely To Hike By 50bps In December.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: Fed Likely To Hike By 50bps In December
Date:    7 hours ago
Fed Likely To Hike By 50bps In December

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
Fed Likely To Hike By 50bps In
December
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 10 Nov 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by liam ward / Unsplash
I. The Fed Has More Work To Do
On October 26, we warned that the Fed could surprise markets with a
25bps rate hike at its October meeting. If the Fed chose to pass on doing
so because the midterm elections were too close, we predicted a 50bps
hike in December. Two days later, the FOMC voted 10-2 to hold the
federal funds rate (FFR) at 3.75%-4.00%, with the two dissenters favoring
a 25bps hike. Yet, in his October 28 presser, Fed Chair Kevin Warsh's

### Page 3

assessment of the economy sounded decidedly hawkish. Economic
growth was robust and broadening, the labor market was in good shape,
inflation remained above the Fed's 2% target, and financial conditions
were hardly tight.
So why did the Fed stand pat?
Warsh argued that financial conditions had tightened, inflation
expectations remained anchored, and stronger productivity growth was
allowing the economy to expand without overheating. With an information-

rich period ahead, the Fed saw little reason to rush. We called that
decision puzzling, comparing it to someone standing in a room with a fire
alarm blaring and smoke pouring under the door, yet insisting on more
evidence before calling the fire department.
Now Fed Governor Christopher Waller is sounding the same alarm.
Speaking in London on Tuesday, Waller said he would support a 25bps
rate hike in December even if inflation improved in October. However, if

October's core CPI increased by around 0.3% m/m or more, as it had in
the previous two months, he argued that a larger move would be
warranted. Specifically, he said that "a 50 basis point hike in the policy
rate should be considered at the December meeting." Waller warned that
moving by only 25bps under those circumstances would jeopardize the
Fed's inflation-fighting credibility.
Economic data released since Waller's remarks suggest that a 50bps hike

in December is becoming increasingly likely. We believe such a move is
warranted, as it would bring the FFR back to our estimated neutral range
of 4.25%-4.50%. Consider the following:
(1) CPI Inflation. October's headline CPI rose by 0.4% m/m, while core
CPI increased 0.3% m/m for the third consecutive month (chart).
Inflationary pressures remain broad-based, with particularly strong
increases in energy, food, and transportation services. Importantly, the

core CPI reading meets Waller's condition for necessitating a 50bps rate
hike in December.

### Page 4

Three forces are contributing to renewed goods inflation: the AI
investment boom, higher energy prices stemming from the Iran war, and
tariffs. In October, prices rose sharply in tariff-sensitive categories such as
furniture & bedding and appliances (chart). However, overall durable
goods prices were essentially unchanged, largely reflecting a 1.2%
decline in used car and truck prices.
Inflationary pressures were also widespread among nondurable goods,

with prices rising 0.5% m/m in October (chart). Recreational reading
materials, housekeeping supplies, footwear, and energy saw particularly
sharp increases. Apparel prices were essentially unchanged, while prices
for computer and software accessories fell.

### Page 5

Another concerning aspect in the inflation data is the persistence of
services inflation, reflecting resilient consumer demand, housing cost
pressures, and the pass-through of higher energy prices. Services prices
rose 0.4% m/m in October, including a 0.4% increase in services
excluding shelter (chart). Postage and delivery, hospital services,
transportation services, and motor vehicle insurance rose sharply.
Owners' equivalent rent also rose 0.3%.

(2) Small Business Sentiment. Echoing the CPI report's fire alarm,
October's NFIB survey showed that the net share of small business
owners planning to raise selling prices jumped to 37%, the highest since
July 2022, while 24% planned to raise worker compensation, the highest
since December 2025 (chart).

### Page 6

(3) Consumer spending. Spending remains remarkably robust. Redbook
same-store retail sales rose 10.1% y/y in the week of November 6, well
above the 2025 full-year average of 5.8% (chart).
(4) Labor market. One of the key reasons consumer spending remains
remarkably strong is the continued strength of the labor market. The latest
ADP data support this assessment. Private employers added an average
of 19,750 jobs per week during the four weeks ending October 24 (chart).

That translates into roughly 86,000 jobs per month, a healthy pace of job
creation that is sufficient to keep the unemployment rate near its current
low level of 4.1%.

### Page 7

clause.

## Document 360: Global Views_ Downside Growth Risks

Source PDF: all report pdf/Global Views_ Downside Growth Risks.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-10-26
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Jan Hatzius 
+1(212)902-0394 | jan.hatzius@gs.com 
Goldman Sachs & Co. LLC
Global Views: Downside Growth Risks
26 October 2026 | 6:45AM EDT
1. So far in 2026, global growth has been remarkably unremarkable.  Both the world 
as a whole and the G3 economies are growing in line with our estimates of potential 
GDP.  Our interpretation is that fiscal easing and the positive impulse from the AI 
boom have offset the negative impulse from the war in Iran and the lingering drag 
from higher US tariffs.

2. Our standing global GDP forecasts project more of the same in 2027, as lower 
energy prices offset fading tailwinds from fiscal policy and AI.  But the balance of 
risks is on the downside.  The most immediate concern is that, far from subsiding, 
energy prices have again climbed sharply on fears of renewed Middle East escalation.  
The prices of gasoline, diesel, jet fuel, and European/Asian natural gas are now near 
or even above their March/April highs, with likely negative effects on consumers and 
energy-importing economies more broadly.

### Page 2

3. Although we don’t expect significant fiscal policy changes in 2027, the consequences 
of large-scale deficits—coupled with AI-linked borrowing and central bank hikes—have 
started to show up more clearly in higher longer-term interest rates.  The moves have 
been mostly orderly, but the increase since early 2026 averages a sizable 100bp across 
DM yield curves.  If sustained, we estimate that this increase will subtract a cumulative 
0.6% from GDP via weakness in housing, consumer durables, and business investment.  
(Only part of the drag is incorporated in our standing forecasts because we expect bond 
yields to pull back as central banks deliver fewer hikes than priced in markets.)

Exhibit 2: Oil Price Risks Are Skewed to the Upside

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research
26 October 2026   2
Goldman Sachs Global Views

### Page 3

4. The growth impulse from the AI boom was set to peak in late 2026 or early 2027 even 
before concerns around the sustainability of the AI investment boom intensified in 
recent weeks.  Although AI investment is still rising rapidly, we expect the increase as a 
share of GDP to slow over the next year.  Alongside the fading boost from last year’s 
increase in US depreciation allowances, this will weigh on capital spending growth, 
especially in the US.  AI has also boosted US consumer spending growth via a positive 
wealth effect worth an estimated 0.4pp (or 0.25% of GDP) over the past year.  This 
impact is likely to decline over the next year, gradually if stocks perform in line with our 
strategists’ reasonably upbeat expectations but more sharply if AI-related stocks 
continue to decline.

5. Our own base case remains that AI investment will ultimately pay off in terms of higher 
productivity and economywide profits.  However, one key channel for this payoff is labor 
market disruption, which has negative near-term macroeconomic effects.  And while DM 
labor markets still look solid in aggregate, we are starting to see clearer signs that the AI 
impact is spreading beyond the technology sector.  Across nonmanufacturing industries, 
the share of US firms that have adopted AI in regular business functions now shows a 
statistically significant negative correlation with employment growth and a statistically 
significant positive correlation with unemployment.

6. The Fed’s recent signals—explicit from Williams/Jefferson and implicit from Warsh—
effectively rule out a hike on October 28.  But the FOMC still looks very likely to deliver 
another 25bp hike to 4-4.25% on December 9 because spot inflation remains too high.  
And while we continue to expect significant disinflation next year on the back of 
reduced impulses from tariffs, energy prices, and AI-related prices, the risks to our 
forecast are on the upside, most obviously in energy.  Higher inflation could result in

7. Like the Fed, the ECB will likely hold rates steady next week and deliver a final hike (to 
2.75%) at the December meeting.  However, the turbulence in the French bond market 
poses somewhat greater downside risks to that call than in the US.  The turbulence has 
tightened financial conditions to a degree that is likely to shave ¼-½pp from annualized 
growth in coming quarters.  And if it intensifies into December, markets might perceive a 
hike as signaling insufficient focus by the ECB on the risk of Euro area fragmentation.  
While the ECB could address this issue by coupling a hike with renewed balance sheet 
expansion, the hurdle for such a move is high, in part because purchases of French 
bonds would inevitably raise concerns about moral hazard with respect to the needed 
fiscal adjustment.

### Page 6

November 5.  Consistent with our view that the UK economy is stronger than widely 
perceived, GDP growth has surprised on the upside this year and the unemployment rate 
declined to 4.7% in August on a 3-month average basis (with the volatile 1-month rate 
plunging to 4.1%).  Rising inflation and the looming household energy price reset in 
January imply a significant risk of further moves, although market pricing of 4+ hikes 
(including November) still looks excessive.  We also expect hikes in Canada and New 
Zealand this week and in Australia next week.

9. Slightly faster than expected growth in Q3 has pushed up our 2026 GDP forecast for 
China to 4.6%.  But most activity indicators are much weaker.  Both property activity and 
fixed asset investment are contracting year-on-year, and while nominal retail sales 
growth picked up to +1.1% in September, large retailers (whose sales are relatively 
better measured) are still posting substantial year-on-year declines.  With domestic 
demand rising slowly at best, sustained GDP growth in the official 4.5-5% target range 
will require ongoing steep increases in the trade surplus.  But the surplus is already at 1% 
of global GDP, and further increases will eat more noticeably into output and 
employment abroad.  Ultimately, China’s trading partners may respond by imposing 
trade barriers that weigh on Chinese exports and GDP, although the measures taken so 
far have been limited.

Exhibit 8: UK Growth Forecasts Have Drifted Higher

Source: Bloomberg, Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research
26 October 2026   6
Goldman Sachs Global Views

### Page 7

10. Our strongest global view is that central banks will deliver fewer hikes than currently 
priced.  In addition, our rates strategists flag underweight positioning, diminished 
technical risks, and risk asset pressures as reasons why the recent rally might extend.  
The market’s narrow breadth makes our equity strategists wary of momentum factor 
exposure, but they still expect the S&P 500 to resume its climb after the midterm 
election, as earnings continue to grow solidly.  Our credit strategists remain cautious on 
IG technology bonds but see scope for some near-term reversal in HY given the scale of 
the recent selloff.  Our FX strategists expect the Japanese yen to act as a hedge against 
further AI jitters, in part because such jitters would shrink US-Japan interest rate 
differentials.  And our commodity strategists recommend targeted exposures to oil 
products and European natural gas to hedge near-term escalation risks alongside 
longer-term exposures to copper, gold, and select power markets to benefit from 
structural supply/demand imbalances.

## Document 397: NZ Q3 Labour market data - Potential RBNZ implications (2026-11-18)

Source PDF: all report pdf/NZ Q3 Labour market data - Potential RBNZ implications (2026-11-18).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-18
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#labour #newzealand
NZ Q3 Labour market data - Potential RBNZ implications
By Kelly Eckhold
Chief Economist, Westpac New Zealand
13:16 November 04 2026 Share
The Q3 labour market statistics were weaker than expected. We discuss potential
implications for the RBNZ noting that a December hike remains more likely than not at this
point.
Q3 Labour market data: potential RBNZ implications
The Q3 labour market data were weaker than RBNZ and our expectations.

### Page 2

Today's labour market data seem unequivocally weaker than expected by either us or the RBNZ. While
likely not a gamechanger for the cycle overall, it will add ammunition to the arguments of the MPC
doves that a pause in interest rate rises will make sense soon - perhaps in December or in the New
Year. It’s likely the case that a bit much was priced into December, so the change in market
probabilities in the wake of today’s data makes sense - although my base case remains that a
December rate rise remains more likely than not.
The message from the RBNZ in the October meeting though was to be careful about pricing in "done
deals" and that the outlook will be data-dependent. As we noted at the time, alongside measures of
inflation expectations, the key domestic data to watch ahead of the RBNZ’s 9 December meeting will
be indicators of what is happening to excess capacity (the output gap) as this will be a key determinant
of the Bank’s assessment of the outlook for inflation over the coming year.
It’s useful to remember in the RBNZ’s forecasting framework that GDP figures are the main
determinant of excess capacity in the economy (i.e., the output gap). The Q2 GDP data told us that the
output gap probably narrowed a bit through Q2, mainly reflecting historical revisions. In light of today’s
labour market numbers, the forecast for growth in Q3 may have some downside risk - but there are
conflicting indicators there, so it’s not that clear. However, it’s probably hard to argue that the level of
excess capacity reduced further in Q3 given today’s data. 
In the eyes of dovish MPC members, the rise in the unemployment rate and other measures important
for the RBNZ's labour market dashboard likely push in the direction of having greater comfort with the
medium-term inflation outlook. The vacancy to unemployment ratio increased a bit, labour constraints
eased a bit, and the unemployment rate has increased. Hence from a labour market perspective it
could be argued that policy could have more time all else equal. The outcome of the election, with the
possibility of a new RBNZ mandate, could increase the prominence of these factors - although this
won’t likely be an explicit factor in the RBNZ’s thinking in December even though it may be in the minds
of individual MPC members.
It could be the case that the argument for increases in the OCR track through 2027 could be
undermined with this data. It’s important to note (probably self-evidently) that there are other data that
will also be relevant. Hence the risk of a pause earlier in the New Year has probably increased with this
data, all else equal. I don't think our forecast peak OCR is really at issue here, but the time taken to get
there could be more flexible. Takes along the lines of "3% is the terminal rate" are very premature and
misplaced, in our view.

A December hike remains more likely than not, but there will be a clear debate to be had between the
MPC hawks and doves.
The forecast peak OCR is likely less impacted than the time to reach that peak. Further pauses in the
tightening cycle could come earlier than currently forecast. 
The outlook for the exchange rate is especially clouded by this data should it take a longer time for
NZ rates to move closer to global norms. 
Watch for updated RBNZ commentary on 10 and 19 November. The Governor’s observation that it
would have been useful to update markets ahead of the October meeting could imply a greater
willingness to comment in the coming weeks.

### Page 3

The outlook for the exchange rate is undermined by this data. I feel very happy about our year-end
target of 79c for the NZD/AUD rate with the risk that a recovery in the NZD takes longer than currently
assumed.
Looking ahead, there are some opportunities for the RBNZ to update markets on their views. Top of
mind is Paul Conway's 10 November speech to the UBS Australasia Conference. The Governor is also
speaking to the Institute of Directors on 19 November. If the RBNZ has something to say, then it might
happen in conjunction with these events (the 10 November speech is titled “How is the NZ economy
tracking”, so it will be difficult for Conway to avoid saying something about how he is interpreting
today’s data). We also note the Governor's comments in the October MPR that it could have been
useful to have been more flexible about updating markets on emerging views of new economic data.
This might increase the likelihood that these events could be used to provide guidance on the RBNZ’s
views of this data (and other emerging events), especially if it feels that the market’s interpretation is at
odds with its own.

Media contact
Kelly Eckhold
Chief Economist
+64 9 348 9382
 +64 21 786 758 
 kelly.eckhold@westpac.co.nz

## Document 421: The AI Trade Is Dead, Long Live The AI Trade!

Source PDF: all report pdf/The AI Trade Is Dead, Long Live The AI Trade!.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-11-18
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: The AI Trade Is Dead, Long Live The AI Trade!
Date:    1 hour ago
The AI Trade Is Dead, Long Live The AI Trade!

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
The AI Trade Is Dead, Long Live
The AI Trade!
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 17 Nov 2026
View in browser
Photo by Frolicsome Fairy / Unsplash

I. The AI Trade Is Dead!
"The King is dead, long live the King!" is a traditional proclamation
announcing a monarch's death while immediately saluting their successor
to show unbroken royal continuity. We think this proclamation applies to
the CEO of Nvidia and the broader AI trade.

### Page 2

Over the past few weeks, we have frequently discussed three key
constraints on the AI investment boom: physical, financial, and political.
Lately, all three have been in play. The AI buildout still needs more
semiconductors and more power. In recent months, the bond vigilantes
have driven up costs for AI borrowers, especially the neoclouds. Then
came the Democrats' midterm sweep, raising fears of growing political
opposition to data center construction.

Now we have to contend with the resignation of Nvidia's CEO Jensen
Huang. The alleged Huang honeytrap scandal adds another layer of
complexity to everyone's outlook for the AI industry.
On the financial front, under Huang, Nvidia has acted as the central bank
of the AI trade, holding $99 billion in equity investments across the AI
ecosystem as of July, plus $25 billion in commitments. It also announced
Wall Street partnerships to mobilize more than $500 billion in third-party

infrastructure financing. But Huang's influence extended beyond these
arrangements. His reputation as the Godfather of AI gave Nvidia-backed
companies a powerful vote of confidence, helping them attract capital from
other investors and lenders. His departure raises questions about both
sources of financial support.
On the political front, the Nvidia-Megaspeed relationship exposed three
potential export-control issues for the US: (1) Intermediaries have been

sending restricted chips to China. (2) China has been accessing overseas
computing capacity remotely. (3) The identities of end-users haven't been
properly vetted. The Commerce Department's latest restrictions aim to
address all three through tighter export controls, remote-access controls,
and due diligence requirements.
Physical constraints could also intensify if China retaliates by restricting
critical mineral exports, potentially disrupting semiconductor production

and the broader AI buildout.
Investors increasingly feared that these constraints could become
roadblocks, bringing the AI investment boom to an abrupt halt.
II. Long Live The AI Trade!

### Page 3

We anticipated buying opportunities in the stock market after the midterm
elections. We see more opportunities than we expected because of the
mounting panic over the AI trade. We view the constraints as speed
bumps that are slowing the AI race. Slowing the pace should reduce the
number of accidents. But the AI investment boom should continue
because the most important variable is demand for compute, which should
keep growing rapidly for the foreseeable future.

Nvidia's latest Q3 earnings and conference call today bolstered our
conviction. Here are our key takeaways:
(1) Another earnings beat. Q3 revenue of $115.8 billion exceeded
expectations of $109.0 billion, while adjusted EPS of $2.68 beat estimates
of $2.47. Data center revenue surged 110% y/y to $107.6 billion,
confirming robust compute demand.
(2) Stronger Q4 outlook. Management guided for Q4 revenue of $130
billion, above the consensus of $125 billion. Nvidia's next-generation Vera

Rubin platform has also begun shipping.
(3) Even stronger FY 2027 guidance. Interim CEO Collette Kress raised
projected revenue growth from 70% to 75%, easing concerns that the
scandal and tighter export restrictions would undermine Nvidia's outlook.
Notably, the forecast assumes no data center compute revenue from
China.
(4) Slowing financing activities. Kress reaffirmed Nvidia's support for
the AI ecosystem while emphasizing more selective neocloud financing.

This will undoubtedly be a speed bump for the neoclouds. We don't expect
a wave of bankruptcies in this space. Instead, slower compute expansion
should boost cloud usage pricing and revenues.
(5) No Jensen comeback. Nvidia's board confirmed that it does not
intend to reinstate Jensen Huang as CEO. Colette Kress remains interim
CEO as the search for a permanent successor continues.
Speed bumps reduce the risk of a boom-bust cycle. They are very useful

when an investment boom risks taking the next corner too fast. They
reduce crash risk.

### Page 4

clause.
Yardeni QuickTakes © 2026 – Unsubscribe

Oil remains under pressure as Middle East supply recovers, while
aluminium tightness keeps nearby spreads elevated. Copper
and iron ore continue to face pressure from a softer demand
backdrop

Ewa Manthey and Warren Patterson
Brent is trading below $92/bbl as improving supply conditions outweigh
continued geopolitical risks in the Middle East
Energy - Oil extends decline as supply
concerns ease
Oil prices extended their decline for a second session, with Brent
trading below $92/bbl as improving supply conditions outweighed
continued geopolitical risks in the Middle East. The physical picture
has improved considerably in recent months. Crude oil and fuel
ﬂows through the Strait of Hormuz rose to 9.1mb/d in 3Q26 from
5.1mb/d in the previous quarter. However, ﬂows remain well below
the 21.4mb/d recorded a year earlier, highlighting that regional
supply has yet to fully normalise, as data from the Energy
Information Administration (EIA) shows.

Other indicators also point to easing near-term tightness. Brent’s
prompt spread has narrowed to around $2.40/bbl in backwardation
from more than $3/bbl a month ago. Meanwhile, speculative
positioning has continued to weaken, with money managers
cutting their net long in NYMEX WTI by 14,719 lots to 70,584 lots
last week, marking a seventh consecutive weekly decline.
Geopolitical risks remain elevated, with continued attacks on
vessels highlighting the vulnerability of regional export

infrastructure. However, improving ﬂows from the Persian Gulf and
expectations for a looser global oil balance are increasingly
oﬀsetting these concerns. We expect improving supply availability
to remain a headwind for oil prices, although any renewed
disruption to ﬂows through the Strait of Hormuz remains the key
upside risk.
Elsewhere, US natural gas prices extended their decline as
improving supply and forecasts for warmer weather weighed on
the market. Production in the Appalachian region appears to be

recovering as producers bring previously withheld volumes back to
the market, while above-average temperatures forecast for parts of
the Midwest and Northeast should reduce near-term heating
demand. However, strong LNG demand should provide some
support, with ﬂows to US export terminals reaching record levels
and limiting the amount of gas available to the domestic market.
Metals - Gold rebounds from recent lows
Gold edged higher this morning after falling to its lowest close since

September 2025 in the previous session. The modest rebound
comes after a sharp sell-oﬀ driven by persistent US inﬂation, higher
Treasury yields and growing expectations that the Federal Reserve
will keep monetary policy tighter for longer. Weakness in
technology stocks has also weighed on bullion recently as investors
reduced exposure to cover losses elsewhere. Still, the prospect of
further Fed tightening remains a headwind for precious metals.
Attention now turns to the minutes from the Fed’s October meeting

for further clues on the outlook for interest rates. We expect gold to

## Document 454: What lessons can we learn to keep European industry competitive from the measures taken by Germany, as the cost of electricity continues to turn the screw_ (2026-10-22)

Source PDF: all report pdf/What lessons can we learn to keep European industry competitive from the measures taken by Germany, as the cost of electricity continues to turn the screw_ (2026-10-22).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-22
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
WHAT LESSONS CAN WE LEARN TO KEEP EUROPEAN INDUSTRY COMPETITIVE FROM
THE MEASURES TAKEN BY GERMANY, AS THE COST OF ELECTRICITY CONTINUES TO
TURN THE SCREW?
Marianne Mueller and Pascal Devaux
issue 19
21 October 2026
The 2022 energy crisis clearly highlighted the issue of electricity prices in Europe — and, more broadly, the issue of energy prices. As
a result, European manufacturers’ competitiveness has been severely undermined when compared with the United States and China.

By reigniting tensions over electricity prices, the conflict that began in the Middle East in late February 2026 further underscores the
continent’s vulnerability to fossil fuel imports, despite the expansion of renewable energy. Faced with an environment of potential sharp
and sudden price rises, European governments do not have the same room for manoeuvre.
While the additional costs borne by European manufacturers are mainly linked to the share of gas in national electricity mixes, we will

first illustrate that, in Germany, grid operation and maintenance costs, as well as taxation, are also contributing significantly to these
costs.
We will then explore the solutions proposed by the German government under the new European measures regulating state aid to
electricity-intensive industries.
Finally, our estimates will show that, while these measures make Germany more competitive against some European countries, they
do not place it on an equal competitive footing with the United States or China. Nevertheless, Germany has made significant progress

in decarbonising and making its electricity mix more flexible from a European perspective. This should help to ensure that electricity costs
are reduced in the medium term.

### Page 2

2
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoInsight 19 / 21 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
worldWHAT LESSONS CAN WE LEARN TO KEEP EUROPEAN INDUSTRY COMPETITIVE FROM THE MEA- SURES TAKEN BY GERMANY, AS THE COST OF ELECTRICITY CONTINUES TO TURN THE SCREW?
GAS: A KEY FACTOR IN THE ADDITIONAL ELECTRICITY COSTS
FACED BY EUROPEAN INDUSTRY
European manufacturers are still paying significantly higher elec -
tricity costs than their American and Chinese competitors. In 2019,

there was a 30% difference between the prices charged to European
electricity-intensive industries (corresponding to the lower end of the
industrial price range) and the average US industrial price. In 2025,
it stood at nearly 100% according to the IEA (2026) 1, after peaking at
an average of 148% between 2022 and 2023. There is currently a 50%
difference with their Chinese competitors2.
The disruption to Russian gas supplies since 2022 is behind the rising

gas prices, particularly as the EU has had to turn to liquefied natural
gas3 (LNG), which is more expensive than gas transported via pipe -
line and, above all, exposes Europe to global gas market conditions.
The growth of LNG has, in fact, intensified competition between the
major consumption regions, primarily Europe and Asia. European and
Asian prices now tend to move more in tandem (Chart 1). However, in
China, the prevalence of coal — a widely available local resource —

and a more regulated electricity price help to keep industrial prices
lower. At the same time, the United States enjoys plentiful domestic
gas, which is produced at a low cost, driven by the sharp rise in shale
oil and gas production over the past decade. By contrast, since 2022,
Europe has been more exposed to the cost of imported gas (90% of its
total consumption, according to Eurostat), while the United States and
China have enjoyed plentiful domestic resources.

Fluctuations and the level of gas prices are passed on to Eu -
ropean electricity prices via the ‘merit order’ marginal pricing
mechanism, with the wholesale price determined by the margi -
nal cost of the last (and most expensive) electricity source called
upon to meet demand.
As a result, gas plays a decisive role, with gas-fired power sta -
tions setting the price in around 40% of cases in Europe4, with si-
gnificant variations depending on national electricity mixes. It is

close to this average in Germany, below it in Spain (26%) and
France (17%), and above it in Italy (73%) 5 in 2025 6 7. Therefore,
electricity prices are still sensitive to fluctuations in gas prices,
even when electricity is not generated from gas (Chart 2). This
pass-through is particularly pronounced when demand rises
and requires the use of more expensive sources, or when supply
constraints arise (for example, during extreme heatwaves that
reduce wind-power generation or disrupt the cooling of nuclear

power plants).
1 Prices – Electricity 2026 – Analysis – IEA
2 The difference may be even larger for other manufacturers who do not receive the same support as electricity-intensive sectors.
3 Imports of Russian gas (via pipeline and LNG) accounted for around 45% of total European imports in 2021, compared with 14% in Q2 2026 (Bruegel, natural gas imports).
4 ACER, 2026, Key developments in EU electricity and gas markets, 2026 Monitoring report.
5 In Italy, in response to sharply rising gas prices in 2026, the government is currently negotiating a potential option of capping the prices of gas supplied to power stations in

order to limit the rise in the wholesale price of electricity. However, this can only be a temporary general mechanism, with the potentially major cost being borne by public funds
and/or households.
6 Eurelectric, 2026, Power Barometer.
7 EMBER, 2026, Latest energy shock reminds Europe of its risky reliance on gas.
8 Energy shock: 2026 vs. 2022 dashboard – 2nd October 2026 – Economic Studies – BNP Paribas
However, for the time being, the 2026 energy crisis is having a far

smaller effect on electricity prices than in 2022. On the one hand, the
2026 peak reached by the European TTF benchmark is still well below
that recorded in 20228.
In addition, there has been significant progress in decarbonising Eu -
ropean electricity mixes since 2022, which has helped to mitigate, on
average, the pass-through of gas prices to electricity prices.
THE ‘MERIT ORDER’ PRINCIPLE IN SETTING ELECTRICITY PRICES

SOME EUROPEAN COUNTRIES HAVE MANAGED TO STAY
COMPETITIVE, BUT GERMANY IS STILL THE EXCEPTION
Electricity prices partly vary due to countries’ electricity mixes
(Chart 3). Electricity-intensive manufacturers in countries where the
share of fossil energy is high face higher costs (Poland, Italy and Ire -
land) (Chart 4). Conversely, countries with a less carbon-intensive mix

(particularly France and Spain in recent times) are less directly ex -
posed to these fluctuations, with costs closer to the United States and
China (Chart 5).
The situation in Germany is paradoxical, as, despite progressively
greening its electricity mix and wholesale electricity prices (averaging
90 EUR/MWh over the 2023–2025 period) and average electricity costs
(across all industrial sectors) in line with the European average, the most
energy-intensive industries face higher costs than the European average

(and even more so compared with international competitors). This is
mainly due to grid costs and a tax burden above EU standards (Chart 6).
9 Eurostat data are used to compare prices with the rest of the EU. However, given that electricity prices are not uniform for businesses at a European level, there may be differences
between Eurostat and national sources. Jäger P., 2025, “Cautionary tale or model for Europe? How member states should react to Germany’s new electricity subsidy”, Hertie School,

Jacques Delors Centre.
10 P. Devaux, 2026, “European Union: low carbon transition and energy sovereignty, a path fraught with obstacles”
In the first half of 2026, for the most electricity-intensive industries,
the difference compared with the European average was 9%, based on
the prices excluding taxes and levies provided by Eurostat. This gap is
mainly due to the cost of accessing the grid. If all taxes and levies are
included, the difference rises to 21% (Chart 7)9.

EUROPE’S APPROACH OF ADOPTING BOTH STRUCTURAL
PROGRAMMES AND TARGETED SUPPORT MEASURES
European programmes to support the low-carbon transition pursue
objectives relating to climate action (reducing greenhouse gas emis -
sions), sovereignty10 (increasing energy self-sufficiency) and competi -
tiveness (reducing the volatility and costs associated with fossil fuels).
The RePowerEU programme, which was launched in 2022 in response
to the energy crisis, primarily aims to reduce imports of Russian gas

and, more generally, to accelerate the low-carbon transition. Although
Europe has reduced its dependence on imports of Russian fossil fuels,
there has still not been enough progress around the energy transition
and cross-border connectivity. This is due to governance constraints

and struggles in implementing the programme in practice within each

State aid is still the main vehicle for this. In response to the 2021–
2022 energy crisis, the European Union temporarily relaxed the rules
on this type of aid in order to support businesses facing rising energy
prices. Any intervention of this kind, provided that it is selective and

could affect trade within the single market, must be authorised in ad -
vance by the European Commission in principle. Since June 2025, the
Clean Industrial Deal State Aid Framework (CISAF) has provided a more
sustainable framework for these kinds of interventions. In particular,
this framework, which runs until the end of 2030, authorises Member
States to grant temporary aid to electricity-intensive businesses to off-
set the cost of electricity. The aid must be targeted and come with

decarbonisation commitments12. Against the backdrop of the crisis in
the Middle East, the temporary METSAF scheme, adopted in April 2026,
helped (in relation to measures falling under the CISAF) to increase the
proportion of additional costs offset by governments from 50% to 70%,
thereby offering additional support to the most at-risk sectors.
GERMANY IS STILL SUFFERING FROM INSUFFICIENT IN-
VESTMENT IN ITS ELECTRICITY NETWORKS, WHICH THE
AID SCHEMES ARE ATTEMPTING TO OFFSET

The issue of electricity costs is a particularly hot issue in Germany,
given the importance of industry to the economy (around 20% of va -
lue added, one of the highest levels in Europe) and its exposure to
international competition. Furthermore, the delay in modernising
electricity infrastructure (only 22.6% of the high-voltage lines planned
for 2025 had actually been completed by that date) is causing bottle -
necks between the production areas in the north and the consump -

11 European Court of Auditors, 2026, “Implementation of the REPowerEU plan must be stepped up”.
12 It applies to the most electricity-intensive activities, which are seeing their competitiveness threatened by international competition, and follows the 4x50 principle: 50% of
electricity consumption is eligible for a subsidy; Of the eligible proportion, the subsidy may not exceed 50% of the wholesale price;
The final wholesale price paid by the company must not be less than €50/MWh;

50% of the aid received must be reinvested in decarbonisation (renewable energy generation, storage and improvements in energy efficiency); Around 90 sectors are covered by this
reform, accounting for approximately 2,000 companies, including in the chemical, steel and paper industries. The scheme, which is strictly regulated, will run for just three years
(from 1st January 2026 to the end of 2028) at an estimated total cost of between EUR 3 and 5 bn, financed by the special Climate and Transformation Fund.

13 Bundesnetzagentur Monitoring Report (2015) and Bundesnetzagentur Monitoring Report (2025). These costs mainly cover the costs of physical infrastructure; operational,
regulatory and compliance costs; and costs associated with using the transmission and distribution network.
14 The Industriestrompreis (industrial electricity price) is the public subsidy mechanism aiming to reduce the price of electricity for some industries.
15 The indirect carbon cost compensation mechanism is a government support measure for electricity-intensive industries, which aims to offset the cost of carbon-emission

allowances for energy producers.
tion centres in the south. As a result, the costs of accessing services
have increased fivefold over 10 years (rising from EUR 1 bn in 2014
to EUR 5.2 bn in 2024 13). At the same time, as we have already seen,
taxation and grid charges are hitting industry hard, with levies on large
consumers far exceeding the European average despite some support
mechanisms being put in place for the most electricity-intensive com-

panies (such as the Bandlastprivileg measure to reduce grid tariffs).
As a result, German industrial production has suffered in particular
due to rising energy prices in recent years, including electricity, with
electricity-intensive industries being especially affected (Chart 8).
In order to cope with these higher electricity costs than the European
average, Germany has been using European support schemes to reduce
the cost of electricity for businesses, particularly since the Merz go -

vernment took office in May 2025. The main scheme — the Industries-
trompreis14, made possible by the CISAF — has been approved by the
European Commission for the 2026–2028 period. While it is less bene-
ficial than the indirect carbon cost (ICC) offsetting included in electri -
city prices15, it has a broader scope, meaning that businesses ineligible
for the ICC can benefit from it. Other measures complement this targe-
ted support by addressing other components of the electricity bill. As a

result, the electricity tax has been set at the European minimum for
manufacturing sectors, while the gas-storage tax has been abolished.
The government is also subsidising grid fees, which should halve them.
Overall, according to our estimates based on 2024 prices, the final
price of electricity for these industries could fall by around a quarter
(to 116 EUR/MWh for industries with annual consumption of between
70 and 150 GWh, compared with a European average of 123 EUR/MWh)

(Chart 9). Germany’s advantage over Central and Eastern European
countries, where electricity costs are generally already higher, looks
set to increase.

However, even when taking all of the subsidies into account, the price
of electricity in Germany would remain higher than the levels seen in
France, Spain or Portugal (and even more so in the United States and
China).
FISCAL MEASURES: LIMITED ROOM FOR MANOEUVRE
There are a number of limitations to these European schemes. For
the country implementing them, lower electricity costs mean that

its industry becomes more competitive against industries from other
EU countries, but this does very little to address the disadvantage that
it faces compared with the United States and China. Therefore, this
measure will unlikely be enough to counteract the loss of market share
seen vis-à-vis the United States and China. In addition, the support
schemes are restrictive (conditional on investment in the low-carbon
transition) and are transitional. Finally, their cost is partly passed on

to the public purse or to consumers who do not receive this support
(in Germany, the cost of the new energy price support measures is
estimated at EUR 14.3 bn in 2026, or 0.3% of GDP16).
Some European manufacturers have proposed abolishing or reducing
the carbon tax as a solution to getting electricity prices down quickly.
In Germany, this tax accounted for 14% of the electricity price in the
first half of 2025, which is higher than the EU average (11%). However,

this proposal is already covered by the indirect carbon cost offsetting
mechanism for electricity-intensive industries exposed to carbon al -
lowance prices. In addition, while adjustments are being made — no -
tably to slow the rate at which allowances are reduced — it seems
highly unlikely that the scheme will be significantly challenged. It is
one of the cornerstones of Europe’s policy to combat climate change,
which has been effective thus far, as shown by the falling greenhouse

gas emissions from industry. More broadly, the gradual introduction of
a carbon border adjustment mechanism (CBAM) should mitigate com-
petition distortions caused by environmental practices17.
IN GERMANY AND THE REST OF EUROPE, HEAVY INVEST -
MENT IS DRIVING UP ELECTRICITY BILLS IN THE SHORT
TERM
In response to the challenge of the low-carbon transition and indus -
trial competitiveness, European governments must strike a balance
between decarbonising the electricity mix and ensuring a supply of

energy that is flexible enough (particularly from controllable sources)
to guarantee stable electricity systems. The integration of an increa -
sing proportion of intermittent renewable energy sources requires
massive investment in transmission and storage infrastructure (statio-
nary batteries). This expenditure, which places a long-term burden on
grid costs, reduces the scope for support schemes (particularly public
ones) to address competitiveness gaps. In Germany, the cost of the

transition is estimated at between EUR 122 bn, according to the go -
vernment (2.8% of GDP), and EUR 200 bn, according to the DIHK18 (4.6%
of GDP), by 2030–2035. If we take the ECB’s assumption19 that govern-
ments will cover around 17% of these investments, the annual public
16 The Industriestrompreis is estimated at around EUR 1.3 bn per year, the subsidy for network charges is projected to amount to EUR 6.5 bn in 2026, the abolition of the gas sur -

charge around EUR 3.4 bn, and the reduction in the electricity tax nearly EUR 3 bn per year. Furthermore, the ICC had cost EUR 2.3 bn in 2024 for costs incurred in 2023.
17 Jean-Luc Proutat, European Union: the carbon tax at the border in six questions, 6 October 2025.

19 ECB, 2025, Investing in Europe’s green future. Green investment needs, outlook and obstacles to funding the gap, Occasional Paper Series, No. 367.

expenditure burden of the transition in Germany would be between
0.5 and 0.8 percentage points of GDP by 2030. However, this budgetary
pressure comes at the same time as three other budgetary increases:
rising social expenditure linked to an ageing population; the necessary
increase to defence budgets to meet NATO’s target of 3.5% of GDP by
2029; and the rising cost of debt-servicing20.
Germany has already made significant progress in maximising its use

of renewable energy, putting it at the head of the pack in Europe. The
country generates 20% of Europe’s renewable electricity (solar and
wind) and accounts for 30% of installed storage capacity (BESS) 21, far
ahead of other European countries (ranking third globally). However,
despite this momentum, Germany’s dependence on gas has not yet
decreased. As a matter of fact, it is only partially offsetting the planned
closure of coal-fired power stations and the phasing out of nuclear

reactors. Furthermore, the intermittent nature of solar and wind power
could prolong this dependence in the short and medium term. In order
to ensure that the German electricity system is flexible, new invest -
ments in gas-fired power plants, supported by public subsidies, are
planned22.

SUBSIDIES ARE HELPING TO ADDRESS THE EMERGENCY
CAUSED BY ENERGY CRISES WITHOUT RESTORING THE
INTERNATIONAL COMPETITIVENESS OF ENERGY-INTEN-
SIVE INDUSTRIES
In response to the impact of energy crises on industrial competitive -
ness, European governments are finding themselves having to strike a
delicate balance between providing short-term support to industries

exposed to increased international competition and continuing — if
not, accelerating — energy transition policies which will result in hi -
gher costs for electricity-network users for several years to come.
Beyond direct economic support measures, which are difficult to sus -
tain long term, four priority areas are emerging:
1/ Demonstrating flexibility in implementing European directives in
order to factor in national specificities and the differing timeframes

between energy crises (which can lead to irreversible industrial conse-
quences) and reforms (which have a longer-term horizon). The relaxa-
tion of the European carbon tax mechanism 23 appears to be a step
towards this.
2/ Continuing efforts to decarbonise the electricity mix, which are still
the main vehicle for sustainably reducing electricity prices for Euro -
pean industries in the medium and long term.
3/ Optimising the use of electricity networks on the demand side, in

particular through smart grids, a flagship element of the Commission’s
COM (2026) 600 reform presented last July24.
4/ Coordinating European electricity networks in order to harness the
complementarities between national systems.
Article completed on 19 October 2026
